ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e143	The background to this case is the evaluation of the corruption prevention work carried out by the Council of Europe’s corruption prevention body, GRECO. The comments made by GRECO were assessed by a sample set up by the Presidency, where all parties were invited to participate, all except the Centre Party and the Green Party. The workgroup's opinions have been reviewed by the administration and the Presidency and have led to the suspension of the day. In addition, the Presidency proposes some amendments aimed at clarifying and improving some of the provisions of the Regulation. In addition, the Presidency follows up the decision of the Grand Duchy of Luxembourg of 12 April of this year, where the Presidency was asked to take the initiative to amend the rules governing the economic register, so that company interests held by the representatives must be listed here, irrespective of their value. This means removing the threshold to register company interests in § 9 of 1 pst. of the capital of the company or 1G in value. You have to register the company interests, whether there are one or a thousand shares, to put it that way. In addition, the name of the company in which they are interested must be entered in the register. The Presidency proposes that it should also be included in the provision that the form of liability of the company should be stated. There is information that helps to inform about financial responsibilities and risks associated with the investment and can therefore also give an indication of the degree of association with the company. The Presidency considers the proposal to comply with GRECO’s recommendation that the registry provide more information on financial commitments. It is then proposed to establish current practice in the second paragraph of Section 9, as follows: Shares held indirectly through a company that is intended to invest in, for example: shares, other securities, commercial property or, where appropriate, shipping shall not be disclosed. It is sufficient to state that shares of the named investment company are held and that this company is intended to invest in other companies. This obligation to register indirect ownership of company interests was introduced in the 2012 Regulations of the Register. The presidency then saw it as natural to limit disclosure. The Presidency said in the Innst. 21 S for 2012–2013: ‘But no detailed explanation may be required in several paragraphs. For example, a reference to a company investing in shares, other securities and/or property in other companies would be sufficient. If the public wants supplementary information, such information could be obtained from other public sources.» This opinion, on the basis of current practice, is also quoted in today’s vote. The reason why the disclosure obligation is intended to be limited to underlying corporate interests owned by an investment company is, of course, not that one would conceal the interests, but that one would avoid excessive and unmanageable registration obligations. The inclusion of the investment company in the register makes it apparent that the company is engaged in investment activities. This is the important information, because it is possible to go to the Register of Accounts — Brønnøysund’s Registers — and get information about the interests of the company that it owns, as it appears from quarterly reports and annual accounts, which must be submitted by obliged undertakings. However, this information is not regularly updated. In addition, such information is available on several of the investment companies’ websites. There are also searchable databases, such as: proff.no, which discloses information about company interests on the basis of information contained in public registers. The majority decided in June 2015 to ask the government to submit proposals for a Norwegian public ownership register. When such a register is in place, real ownership information could be made even more accessible to the public. So those who think it's possible to hide their interests behind an investment company will be taken into account. Then why not require each representative to include this information in the register? Because there will be a requirement that can be very difficult to keep up to date for each representative. It may be about investment portfolios that change frequently, perhaps from day to day, whose investment in shares and securities is part of the company's objective. In addition, it will not provide further information on the interests of the representative than is already available. This will, in fact, be a double registration. It is also companies that have portfolios of shares on the stock exchange and that are indirectly owned to hold shares in that company. It could be a production holding. The same problem will arise here if registration is to be required in several paragraphs. It will simply be information that changes rapidly and that may be difficult for indirect ownership. There are also pension-saving schemes that are a mixture of banking and equity saving – there is also an example of an indirect interest that it would not be practicable to explain in the second subparagraph. Section 8 proposes that property in industry, owned by a company in which a representative owns or is a joint owner, should be reported to the register. The same company will also be subject to registration under Section 9 on company interests. This becomes an unnecessary double registration and it should be sufficient to make visible the economic interest by registering the interest in the company. Therefore, the obligation to register the property in question under Section 8 is proposed. However, non-residential or joint-owned commercial property will continue to be subject to registration under Section 8. The Presidency proposes that the form of ownership be specified also by registration under Section 8. Under the rules in force, interests shall not be entered in the register of amounts, values or quantities. Following on from the recommendation of GRECO to list several of the matters subject to registration with amounts, the Presidency proposes that remuneration for autonomous tasks above NOK 50000 be declared by the amount. The same applies to financial assistance which a representative may receive in addition to his remuneration, provided that the aid exceeds DKK 50000 per year. Furthermore, it is proposed to include remuneration as an economic benefit subject to registration in Section 7. This applies primarily to remuneration received in connection with the transfer of an establishment and paid over several years depending on the performance of the establishment. Certain benefits from former employers are subject to registration under Section 5. It shall now be explicitly mentioned in the provision that this includes loans. The Presidency proposes that attention and anniversary gifts from its own batch or batch group should not be subject to registration. Since suggestions have been made on the contrary, I would like to remind you that the point of the register is to be open about matters that might create suspicions of bonds that might affect the representative's work on matters. We assume that gifts from our own group or party organisation hardly fall into this category. The connection to donors should be transparent and without potential for conflict of interest. It is therefore proposed that attention and anniversary gifts from its own party organisation be exempted from the registration requirement for gifts in Section 11, but of course it is not prohibited to mention whether you follow it most safely. The obligation to register duties and financial interests is the same for representatives and alternates. The Presidency, however, proposes that alternate representatives be exempted from the obligation to specify autonomous, revenue-bearing tasks above NOK 50000. Any self-contained revenue-bearing activity alongside the pooling yard is assumed to be an adjunct that a permanent meeting representative may choose to perform, while for an alternate there will probably be a principal source of income, which cannot be eliminated. With this, I recommend the presidency's setting.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e182	It is good that the presidency now follows up the so-called GRECO recommendations, which it has been working on over some time, but where one has also very quickly followed up the decision that the Storting made on 12 April of this year, which reads: ‘The majority requests the Presidency to take the initiative in amending the Rules of Procedure on the register of the duties and financial interests of the EEAS, so that all company interests, irrespective of their value, are entered in that register.’;» I think the presidency has made an unwise assessment when they have proposed in Section 9 the exemption for investment companies that they have made, as a result of a practice that has been followed since 2012. The central reason for this is that investment companies are a very common way of organising a portfolio of shares today. Especially if you have a significant portfolio of shares, it is common. One then risks being in the situation that the most significant ownership interests and portfolios will not be in Storting’s register, while the smaller – and perhaps less interesting – privately owned portfolios will be there. As I said, many people use investment companies. It's a common approach, partly because it can make it easier to organise a significant portfolio, but also because it can have tax advantages, which the presidency is probably familiar with. It is also the case, as Dagbladet has written, that even in Stortinget – naturally, I would say – representatives do it that way. I do not suspect in any way – as the representative did not – that there is a proposal to hide a portfolio. But I think it would be unwise because the leftover impression may still be that some stock portfolios – and then most of them – will not be in the register. I believe that there is no doubt a breach of the intention of the decision taken on 12 April. I am aware that it follows the practice that has been in place since 2012, but the decision taken in April of this year came after it in time and may be difficult to understand in a different way than to expect that it would apply to different types of investment, saying, ‘so that all company interests, regardless of their value, are listed in this register’. In the light of this, we propose a different wording in Section 9 which also includes investment companies, of course not about one, for example. has unit trusts or other types of funds, but the type of company which is a normal way of organising just such interests as were the subject of the decision of 12 April. If they think it is foolish to adopt a different wording from that of the presidency, we also make a subsidiary proposal, which reads: ‘The vote will ask the Presidency to come back with proposals for amendments to the Rules of Procedure on the register of the duties and financial interests of the EEAS, ensuring that all company interests, irrespective of their value, are entered in this register.’ In that case, it will give you an opportunity to look at the wording that best takes care of it. Then we also propose a different amount limit in the proposals for amendments to the third paragraph of Section 3 and Section 7. Paragraph 3 applies to fees that we accept, and Section 7 applies to different types of assistance from organisations, companies or others. The setting controls whether the limits are set to NOK 50000 - from one dealer. That's a pretty big amount. This means, for example: one can receive very large fees, each of which is less than NOK 50000. If I can use myself as an example, since I have a column in a newspaper that – if I have counted correctly – I can get just under $50,000 for this year: I don't want to see it's untimely if there's a requirement that I should give up that I'm a splitter in the paper. Or if a person is thinking of a different type of assistance – let’s say that a large Norwegian employee organisation, for example, would provide substantial support for various types of help to political work: I would not see that it would be unreasonable to give up – if it were just under $50,000. It's also so that the sums we talk about there can be big. So we're going to suggest that we also use a limit of NOK 2,000, which is the gift limit that we use when it comes to giving things up in the Stortinges register. When it comes to the question of registration obligations for gifts from the party organisation, I would like to express my full agreement with the representative Hansen. It may hardly be about bonds that create problematic loyalty – perhaps one might rather say the opposite, that it may be positive for partial loyalty to have a new gift. But maybe that illustrates the difference in the thinking. We think that it is wise to have openness, unless there are particular arguments against openness, i.e. the principle on which the public law of Norway is based. ‘More publicity’ is the principle that usually has a lot of support in this room, but here it is perhaps – I get the impression – based on a opposite principle, that we do not need openness in this, unless someone has specifically demonstrated that there is a problem. I don't think it'll cause much fear if we have to give it up. I myself have in some contexts taken gifts from the party organisation, in special settings. I have no great fear of having to give up. That's my starting point. However, I would say that there is a relatively insignificant question in this context, which more illustrates a different set of thoughts. On the other hand, what is not a minor question is whether a very common way of organising a portfolio of shares, namely in an investment company, shall be part of the rules or be kept out of it. I'm taking up the suggestions that SV is behind.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e327	The Presidency is presenting today its position on the regulation of allowances for the gross domestic product representatives and the members of the government as decided or proposed by the Enlarged Board of Directors. The Grand Duchy’s Wage Commission was set up on 21 June 1996 by the Stortinget. In the new law on remuneration for wholesalers, applicable from 1 January 2012, previous legislation and practices are continued. The Presidentship of the Grand Duchy appoints the Chairman of the Board of Directors and two other members for four years. The Additional Rules for the Commission’s activities, set out by the Presidency on 21 June 1996, as amended, read as follows: ‘The Commission’s task under the decision of the Storting is to determine, with the consent of the Storting, the annual remuneration of the representatives and members of the Government.’ Let me stress that the assessment and determination of the Wage and Wage Commission is done on a free basis, regardless of the presidency's assessment, and it has been important that they are an independent body that does this in relation to wage developments elsewhere in society. Remunerations are determined either at specified crowning amounts or in relation to other rates or amounts. The Presidency takes note of the Commission's decision on remuneration and promotes a unanimous proposal that the Storting should determine the remuneration proposed by the Enlarged Bureau. The Commission has obtained information from several teams to monitor wage developments in Norwegian working life, both from private and public activities. The Commission also refers to the agreement on the level of settlement of the year which, in previous years, the Wage and Wage Commission has focused on the expected annual wage growth at the year’s settlement. The suspension of the Wage and Wage Commission represents an annual wage increase in 2016 for the Wage and Wage Representatives and members of the Government of 2.4 p.m. This is based on estimated annual wage growth in both the state and local government as well as in the private sector. We note that in the end of the year, the Wage and Employment Commission highlights that social developments in the last year, with increasing unemployment and falling oil prices, have contributed to a more moderate wage settlement in 2016 than in previous years. The year-to-year settlement framework of 2,4 p.m. is based on the proposals of the Wage and Wage Commission and, in this context, the suspension of the Presidency is recommended.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e381	Media pluralism is important for the preservation of democratic, cultural and linguistic values. The emergence of digital communication has made it more demanding to prevent unhealthy power concentration through regulation. It is therefore important to regulate media ownership that is adapted to modern media reality. In the proposal, the Ministry proposes to repeal the Media Ownership Act. There is a great deal of consensus in this matter, and the proposal is supported by a joint committee. The size has recently adopted changes to the Competition Act, which is important when we repeal the Media Ownership Act. We believe that the Competition Act, with the amendments that have now been adopted, will take account of media pluralism in the context of media acquisitions and mergers. The proposal also proposes a new law on transparency of media ownership that maintains the tasks of media oversight relating to transparency of media ownership by the public. The Committee agrees with the Ministry that this could contribute to stressing the importance of transparency in the ownership of the media industry. In its recommendation, the Labour Party proposes the introduction of a specific provision of the Competition Act which allows the assessment of media pluralism in addition to the purely competitive considerations of media concentration. I assume that the representatives of the Labour Party themselves will present the proposal. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e408	The government decides it will. ‘promoting good news production and a wide-ranging public conversation in the digital media society of the future’. The aim is therefore to ‘stimulate innovation and innovation, while respecting the traditions and basic values of the free press’ in Norway. In the case of ownership adjustments in concrete terms, the government states that it wishes to adapt the limitations of ownership of media to the desire to ensure diversity. I have understood that the Media Authority will continue to play a role in contributing to the transparency and knowledge of ownership in Norwegian media. Would the content and profile of a newspaper be equal to whether it is LO, Life's Word, Trygve Hegnar or Berlusconi who owns the paper? Does it matter to democracy whether they control all media covering policies and societal development in a region or throughout Norway? The decisions over which the owners have influence affect the content of the media. For example, it is the owners who employ the editor in charge that define the economy and the framework of resources and make a number of strategic choices for the business. However, direct or indirect influence by the owners is not easy to detect. How, for example, to do this. detect whether a newspaper avoids writing abusively about its own owner? So ownership concentration in the media can lead to a restriction of freedom of expression. In order for an opinion to be read or heard, there must be available channels where the opinion is not available. With the Internet, we have had a situation characterised by an abundance of speech and information. The fact that everyone can now speak in principle can be seen as a strengthening of democracy. At the same time, it is not arbitrary for the pervasiveness of whether the message is promoted on Twitter, in Dagsrevyen or in the locality that is alone in its area – or whether it is called Jens Stoltenberg or Solveig Torsvik. There is still a high and stable uptake of traditional media, and the possibility of producing content does not automatically give rise to influence. We see that there is hierarchical ranking within some media, and some are considered more important than others. In the edited media, there is no general right to access. That's why this is where ownership regulation has been most important. Research work in Norway and internationally indicates that the need for regulation is increasing rather than decreasing as a result of technological developments. Increased concentration and consolidation in the industry, the emergence of new bottlenecks and new ways of locking media users to e.g. software, makes ownership still important. A recent example in one of our neighbouring countries illustrates what can happen when powerful people gain great media control. Last summer, the British newspaper The Guardian revealed that employees of the competitor News of the World, owned by Murdoch, have for a number of years been listening to telephone calls from both public and private people. Can you imagine that such relationships would be revealed in a newspaper with the same owner as the News of the World? Nope. In the wake of the case, several measures have been taken in the United Kingdom, among others. examines the relationship between the Murdoch family and British politicians. Many suggest that mutual close ties have had political and journalistic consequences. We know who owns Norwegian media today, but we can't say anything about the future. Changes in ownership take place quickly and can have major consequences. It is difficult to reverse the concentration of owners in retrospect if it is found to have negative consequences. An argument that has been promoted against the law is that ownership restrictions lead to the favouring of non-resident owners over Norwegians.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e448	The government sees it as a central task to protect about media pluralism. Promoting media pluralism is an objective of which there is broad political outreach and which is enshrined in both the Constitution § 100 and Article 10 of the European Convention on Human Rights. The aim of media diversity includes a variety of and widespread ownership in Norwegian media. It doesn't matter who owns the channels we use to speak, discuss, collect information and orient ourselves in society. But that's not the topic of today's discussion. Today's debate is about how the objective of media pluralism can be promoted in a way that is effective and that does not impose unnecessary burdens on industry. Today, media acquisitions and mergers are dealt with by two supervisory bodies under two different legislations. There is a poor use of resources both for the state and for the media sector. I am therefore pleased that a unanimous committee joins the proposal under consideration today. By repealing the Media Ownership Act, the supervisory responsibility of the Competition Authority is brought together, and actors are given one oversight and one regulation to deal with. It will make merger control in the media area both more cost-effective and more effective. When assessing a purchase or merger, competition authorities will already consider the impact on both price and product quality. The importance of media diversity is an important part of this quality assessment. The size has recently adopted changes to the Competition Act which will make the law even better suited to ensuring media pluralism. That is why I see no reason for a new provision on media pluralism in the Competition Act as proposed by the minority. At the same time, time has passed from the Media Ownership Act. The law pre-defines markets and ownership limits based on what the media sector looked like at the end of the 1990s. However, since then, the media industry has undergone major changes in technological developments, changing user habits and new areas of competition, and the changes continue to increase at an increasing pace. I would therefore like to stress once again that I am pleased that there is an overall committee that joins the proposal to repeal the Media Ownership Act. The media act as channel guards by influencing who and which opinions are released into the public domain. In order to determine the information provided through the media, it is important to know where it comes from and what interests are behind. The government has therefore committed itself to maintaining the responsibility of the Media Regulatory Authority to ensure public transparency in ownership of the media sector. It is a media policy task that it is not natural to add to the Competition Authority, and I note that a Joint Committee also supports that proposal. I'm glad.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e671	It is an important topic that is addressed in the proposal for a representative, and I share the Committee's view that it is serious for young people to experience body pressure. The historical effort of health stations, school health services and community psychologists is important in this respect. The Committee considers that there is a need for more research into the impact on the health and self-image of children and young people and on the reasons why children and young people experience body pressure. Furthermore, it points to the need for more knowledge about what protects children and what gives them the strength to cope with pressures, demands and expectations. The Institute of Public Health can point to an increase in mental distress among children and young people and also believes that more knowledge of causes is needed. That is why I am pleased that the Health Directorate has commissioned research from different environments on stress, stress, mental health, loneliness and bullying. There will also be important sources of knowledge required by the Committee. I also look at the possibility of using research resources from my department in this field. Municipalities are in a unique position to also work with prevention, health promotion and early diagnosis and intervention. Psychiatric health problems among children and young people are a challenge. In a short time, the Ministry of Health and Health will present a youth health strategy. It is an explicit goal for fewer young people to experience body pressure. The overall goal is for young people to have the best conditions to live a good life and to experience mastering physically, mentally and socially. A proposal has been made by the member of the Committee of the Christian People's Party to amend the Marketing Act. The government sees that there are marketing and body pressure challenges. However, I am sceptical about the proposal to ban advertising that could create body pressure. What kind of marketing is it that creates body pressure? Are those reticent pictures? Are there images of models using brown cream or removing hair? It will be difficult to set the boundaries between what can, or cannot, create problems. Unfortunately, we can't always ban everything we don't like. Advertising that creates body pressure may already be prohibited under Section 2 of the Marketing Act, provided that the measure also infringes fundamental moral or gender equality. I ask questions about whether further regulation of this type of marketing would be useful. I also notice that the Country Group of Health Sisters in the hearing stated that the labelling of reticent advertising will have a limited effect and will be difficult to regulate. I would also like to point out that a ban needs to be carefully assessed against Norway’s EEA legal obligations. I am thinking in particular of the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive, which limits how we regulate advertising. If a prohibition does not conflict with the EEA obligations, the next question will be whether it is appropriate to regulate this in the Marketing Act. In this respect, I would like to mention that today's marketing law is primarily intended to protect the financial interests of consumers, not public health. Therefore, I believe that the most important thing we can do now for children and young people is to invest in good health and to strengthen the young people's own ability to resist pressure.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e771	In a short time, the phenomenon of ‘body pressure’ has slipped into the daily language and gained status as our new major health challenge. It has, for example, also attended Question Time of the Grand Duchy on several occasions, where politicians have agreed that something needs to be done here. That's good, and it's also good that it's across the party boundaries. I think we're taking the challenges seriously. The phenomenon of body pressure has also grown enormously in the number of media outlets. A review shows that there has been a major change in production. In 1990, body pressure was associated with fanatical involvement of the body. Today it is described as something beyond ourselves, which increases from week to week, which can cause eating disorders, depression, anxiety and other mental disorders. Furthermore, we see that experts, journalists, politicians and other adults who communicate body pressure, speak as if it were not for ourselves, and that it is the youth who is most vulnerable. But the youth himself is equally unambiguous in it. A girl who wrote in the Evening Post says, ‘We have reached the point where you are obliged to feel body pressure.’ Our starting point is that body pressure is something that is created between people. It is maintained and reinforced by talking and writing about it, and physical disorders such as eating disorders need not have any connection to body pressure. Body press is something that occurs and unfolds among people, but this is not what body pressure is referred to in large headlines that I have read. On the contrary: the media, politicians, these experts, non-governmental organisations and others make body pressure a major nuisance, especially among young people. Body press has therefore become something that needs to be combated in order to get rid of everything that is responsible for it, and there is a lot that needs to be controlled: the newspapers write about bloggers who are perceived as the recipe for body press. The teachers are quoted in the media as saying that joint showering can be body pressure. The sanitary women claim that reticent images are body pressure. When school swimming needs to be changed because there is a certain risk that students may experience body pressure, they may also become aware that wearing bathing clothes can be disgusting. I think you need a different understanding of the body – instead of panic and anxiety, people, especially young people, can be encouraged to experience being a body in the world.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e960	As case spokesman for two cases, I choose to take one at a time. The first one is Prop. 103 L for 2015–2016, which applies to changes in the Kindergarten Act and the Training Act. I would first of all like to thank the Committee for a thorough and important work on a legislative change that applies to the very least and to those who have special needs. There is a joint committee that is concerned that all children consume a good childcare provision, regardless of when one is born or if one needs special arrangements for different reasons. When it comes to special educational assistance, I would like to point out that this helps to ensure that children receive the help they need to deal with problems and challenges, such as: associated with language and behaviour, at an early stage. This help may also help to reduce the risk that the child will need special education later in the course of education. The design of the assessment theme and content of the Training Act is to a large extent linked to special education for pupils and not children in need of special pedagogical assistance. The Barnhage Law Committee proposed in NOU 2012:1 for the benefit of children, therefore changes in the content of the expert assessment. In the proposal, the Ministry, on behalf of the Government, proposes that the legislation on children under the compulsory age of training be moved from the Training Act to the Kinderhagen Act. This means, among other things: that the right to special pedagogical assistance and the right to sign language training will be governed by the Law on kindergarten. Most children receiving special educational assistance go to kindergarten. The government believes it is an advantage that the legislation on children under the age of compulsory education is being incorporated into the same law. This will provide a more logical location for the code and provide a more holistic and user-friendly code. The Ministry, on behalf of the Government, proposes in the proposal some changes to the current law. One of the proposals is that the PP service should support kindergartens in the development of skills and organisation. The proposal means that kindergartens can be helped to develop the ordinary provision of children with special needs. With the assistance of the PP service, kindergartens can be better placed to put their efforts in place early. The Government also proposes that the local authority’s obligation to provide facilities for children with disabilities should be laid down in the Children’s Homes Act. In addition, the Ministry proposes that children and parents be given the right to appeal to the county council in the municipality's decision on the organisation. In addition, the government suggests that the conditions for eligibility for sign language training be changed. The change means strengthening the right to language training for children under the age of compulsory training. The Government also proposes that decisions by the local authorities on special pedagogical or sign language training should be binding until a new decision has been taken by the local authority. The proposal will contribute to children no longer experiencing interruptions in special educational assistance or language training. In this proposition, the Ministry of Knowledge, on behalf of the Government, also proposes changes to the laws aimed at helping parents and children to have shorter waiting time for nursery facilities. It is proposed that children born in September or October should be entitled to kindergarten from the month in which they reach the age of one year. In addition, the proposal contains some other changes to the Kindergarten Act. These are all changes that lead us to a step in the right direction for all children to have the right to babysitting from the month when they reach the age of one year. Parents with children who have decisions on special pedagogical or sign language training are also entitled to exemption from parental payment at the time the child receives assistance or training. One of the objectives of the scheme is to contribute to high levels of childcare participation for children in need of such assistance. 2015 figures show that over 96 pst. by children aged three to five years in Norway, are in kindergarten, and by children who have decisions on special educational assistance, there is also a high level of childcare participation. The transfer of the legal home for the right to special pedagogical assistance from the Training Act to the Kinderhagen Act has previously been proposed. by the Barnehagelov selection in NOU 2012:1, Best for the children, and by the Midlyng selection in NOU 2009:18, Right to learn. Then report. St. 24 for 2012-2013, the Future Children's Home, was discussed in the Family and Culture Committee in spring 2013, and supported the proposal for the transfer to the Children's Homes Act. It is also important to stress that children under the compulsory training age who are not in kindergarten are still entitled to special educational assistance as they are today. The right to special pedagogical assistance applies regardless of whether a person is in kindergarten or not. It is also important to point out the need for good cooperation with other relevant municipal services, during the health stations of this municipality. The Government's proposal continues the legalisation of the local authority's responsibility to ensure that the right to special pedagogical assistance is fulfilled. One also suggests that the Children's Court Act provides that when parents ask for special pedagogical help for their children, the municipality has an obligation to ensure that the PP service produces an expert assessment, and that the municipality then takes a decision. A consensus committee supports this. It is important to point out that disabled people are guaranteed the right to participate in society by national law and international conventions in the same way as those with disabilities. This means that children with disabilities should be able to participate in kindergartens similar to others. In Norway, approx. 60,000 children were born annually. All newborns in Norway are screened for hearing loss by a simple test in the maternity ward. The incidence of hearing loss in newborns is 1 to 2 per million. In addition, children will be given hearing loss at a later time. The number of children with hearing impairment in kindergarten is unknown, and there is generally little knowledge about this group of children and the provision of children in kindergarten. But since about 97 pst. Of all four-year-olds in Norway, there is reason to believe that most children with hearing impairment also go there. The provision of children with hearing impairment is organised differently in municipalities. Some municipalities have created their own kindergartens, which are specially designed for children with hearing impairment, where a sign-language environment is offered. In some cases, the provision of skyscrapers is necessary to enable the child to benefit from the provision of special pedagogical assistance or sign language training. The availability of the children in question may be far from home, and it may be a particular burden for parents to carry the child to help or training. Therefore, the rights of children entitled to special pedagogical assistance and language training should be extended free of charge. The government shows in the political platform that it will work for increased flexibility in the nursery. Kindergarten is an important social institution for a large number of people, and the right to kindergarten gives children the opportunity to participate in a community and to develop independently of the economic or social situation of their parents. For many parents, the right to kindergarten is decisive for their economy and the organisation of everyday life. I am pleased that we have a government that wants to make sure that several children can benefit from the right to babysitting, and that the government proposes to give more children the right place by reducing the waiting time for parents with children born in September or October. This is a step in the right direction. Then to Prop. 33 L for 2015–2016 on changes to the Kindergarten Act, which deals with supervision, etc. As rapporteurs, I would like to thank the Committee for its thorough and important work on a change in the law that applies to the very least, and that concerns most children more and more. Norwegian kindergartens keep a high quality and make important contributions to a childhood filled with mastery and meaningful association. As a mother, a grandmother and a big-time politician, I am happy to represent a committee that puts the contents of the nursery on the agenda with changes in the nursery law. On behalf of the Government, the Ministry of Knowledge has presented the proposal for amendments to the Children's Homes Act on the supervision, documentation and assessment, the introduction of learning kindergarten teachers and the clarification that the Ministry can provide a framework for the content and tasks of kindergartens. The government suggests that the State should be allowed to supervise kindergartens. The proposal meets the government's objective of introducing independent supervision in the nursery sector. In addition, the Government proposes that the County House of County to supervise the use of financial means by the municipality should be clarified in the Children's Gardens Act. This is a follow-up to Prop. 98 L for 2011-2012, where the Ministry announced that this assessment would be carried out in future legislative work. In this proposition, the Ministry, on behalf of the Government, proposes that the rules on State supervision and guidance be separated into its own destinations. It is clarified that the supervision of the municipality as a child is a purely statutory body and that the supervision is to be carried out in accordance with the municipal law. In order to ensure early work and the provision of childcare facilities for child groups and the individual child, the government proposes that the Childcare Act should clarify the need to document and evaluate educational provision. The bill means that Kinderhagen will have a clear place to handle personal data and will ensure that documentation and assessment will last for the protection of the privacy of children. The Ministry also proposes that the Kindergarten Act should be amended in such a way as to replace the notion of ‘preschool teacher’ with the term ‘childcare teacher’. It is also suggested that the Children's Court Act should specify that the Ministry may prescribe the content and tasks of the nursery. The choice of parent is important. Parents should be able to choose if they want to benefit from kindergarten, and if they do, they should be able to choose between different kindergarten options and have an impact on the way their children's nursery is operated. Kindergarten security is particularly important. Parents should feel assured that the one-year-old or the five-year-old is comfortable and safe in all the ten-year-old moms and dads at work. When it comes to the local authority's ability to investigate matters before the County Council has taken action, I show that the government in the bill has reason to believe that if the municipality as a child has not been involved in matters where the County Council is asked to supervise, the municipality should be given the opportunity to investigate the matter and to have the possibility to rectify the situation itself before state supervision is taken into account. The Education Directorate has also asked the government to provide information on what can be expected from the follow-up of the municipality when the County Government sends a case to the municipality. The municipality as a child will be able to benefit from both supervision and guidance to investigate and ensure compliance with legal rules. If a case has not already been assessed by children's memory, as a general rule, it would be natural for the County to forward a request for supervision to the municipality. The government will then leave it up to a local discretion to evaluate which tool is most appropriate in the individual case. A joint committee has also noted that Barneombodet has pointed out the need to be able to derogate from the general rule that the municipality should be given the opportunity to investigate the matter before starting State supervision. The Government considers that the County State should be able to put into effect supervision on an exceptional basis without the municipality having examined the case, for example. where the municipality does not investigate a matter within a reasonable period of time, or where the cases are particularly serious. The Committee refers to the fact that, by means of the Kindergarten Act, the municipality has been given the right to impose on orphanage owners the correction of unwarranted or illegal circumstances. Furthermore, the municipality may adopt the time limit or permanent closure of the operation if the time limit for complying with the obligation is not complied with or if the situation is not corrected. If the municipality detects a breach of the requirements imposed on the use of public credits and parental payments, the municipality can use financial means of response. The Committee has also considered whether it is necessary for the State to have the same sanctioning powers as the local authority in its supervision as a child. In order to achieve effective supervision, it is necessary for the County to be able to act with the same weight and efficiency as the municipality and to respond to child care violations. The Committee points out that the Government proposes that the State be given the right to access documents and nursery facilities to the extent necessary for the supervision of kindergarten. The Committee sees that the proposal provides the County Council with the corresponding right of access to documents and childcare facilities to the municipality. The government does not propose any change of reality in the current law when it relates to the responsibility of nursery owners for the fact that the nursery has a documentation and assessment work in accordance with the requirements of the nursery law and the regulation on the framework plan for the content and tasks of the nursery. Kindergarten owners fulfil their responsibilities in cooperation with the pedagogical staff of the nursery. The Kindergartengesetz stated that kindergarten owners should operate the enterprise in accordance with current laws and regulations. Kindergartenowners are thus given the responsibility that the nursery business complies with the requirements laid down in the Children's Homes Act. The Data Protection in School and Kindergarten report of the Data Protection Authority discusses how internal control of personal data should be enhanced. The report indicates that there is an ongoing problem that there are no satisfactory procedures for complying with the Data Protection Act and the personal data requirements, and this applies in particular to the transparency, consent and risk assessment of digital tools dealing with personal data. In any case, it is important that a risk assessment be carried out in kindergarten to ensure that information is not compromised and that there is a need to clarify responsibilities in relation to the controller of personal data. This responsibility has been added to kindergarten owners, and I would like to clarify the responsibility of kindergarten owners for training them when this is the case. A Joint Committee does not want defined standards for the development of children to be developed in the context of a new framework plan. All children shall be assessed on the basis of their potential for development with regard to individual differences between children and the wide range of children with different abilities. The nursery shall not set specific targets for single children without a clear justification. The objectives shall be set in such cases in cooperation with parents and any cooperation parties outside the nursery.

Both the legal text and the propositions have become a patchwork of conflicting signals and leads, and it is very unclear to what extent it will function as a management tool. As we understand it, we don't put the nursery in a lawless state for that reason. Kindergartens are still subject to the Data Protection Act. We do not want the sentences in the government parties' legislative proposals on the routine of documentation. This implies a legal obligation to document and, with the text of the proposition and the notification, this becomes a wrong signal to the sector. If there is a need for a legal framework for the processing of personal data to be maintained, it is enough to mention this only, rather than ‘the routine of documentation’. The background for our skepticism here is, among other things, that the Government of the Proposition does not draw a distinction between documentation, assessments and surveys, etc., as pointed out in our remarks. Then we are very sceptical about the introduction to the comment that seeks to make sure that there will be no changes to the current law and framework plan. We believe a problem today is that today's law and framework plan are interpreted differently. In the view of many people, much of today’s practice in kindergarten is far from the intention of the framework plan. Therefore, we absolutely do not think it reassuring to write: «Today, in line with today's framework plan, systematic observation, documentation and assessment of both the activities of kindergarten and of single children.» This may be true, but it is equally true that there are also practices that are absolutely not in line with what we understand as the intention of today's framework plan. We are in doubt whether it is desirable to legitimise such practices through the new proposal. The Christian People's Party believes that the government's proposal for a new section 2 is better than the government's proposal, but the Christian People's Party will vote to ask for any new proposal from the government, if such is the case, that there is a legal basis for processing personal data. We feel that there are too many ambiguities in what's going on here today, that we want to vote for something else. The Christian People's Party is voting for the government's proposal for a supervisory regime, provided that this is evaluated after two years, and if the evaluation shows that the local authority's supervisory responsibilities are not respected in a good and quality manner, the road will be short here on the Stortinget to propose independent supervision. The Christian People's Party also promotes this proposal: ‘The government asks the Government as soon as possible, and at the latest in the autumn of 2016, to promote a case for the Stortinget where the government’s work on the standard of living and child care teachers’ standards is being prepared.’ So, as I said, the government has presented a report on quality in kindergarten that does not refer in one word to the Christian People's Party's opinion is the most important thing for the quality of kindergarten, staffing standards and kindergarten teachers – enough adults and enough kindergarten teachers. The Christian People's Party believes this is absolutely fundamental, because with enough kindergarten teachers, we guarantee quality through competence and knowledge. The government solution is to try to ensure quality through standards and standardisation. Norms don't create quality, and children don't get better at measuring. Good enough quality in all kindergartens we ensure with demands for enough adults to work all day long and with demands for enough kindergarten teachers in all kindergartens. The Christian People's Party will not have language norms, dividend requirements, more screening and documentation. We therefore promote these proposals, and hope that the majority of the Grand Duchy will vote in favour of the children: ‘The government is asking not to draw up guidance standards for the linguistic basis that children should have with them from kindergarten.’ ‘The government is asking the Ministry to clarify the objectives of the kindergarten’s work in language and to set targets on how the nursery is to work in the field of play, care and social skills.’ It is about not setting targets for each child. The work of kindergarten is to be focused on in the framework plan, not on the performance of the individual child. We listen to the professionals who say that target management of young children will kill learning and work against their purpose. The Christian People's Party is pleased that the Stortinget today gives a clear message that we want to continue the Norwegian and Nordic kindergarten tradition. For the Christian People's Party is one of the main pillars of kindergarten policy: Don't make kindergarten a school! Let care and play touch the sector and let learning come through games! The Christian People's Party has great faith in the Nordic kindergarten tradition, where the self-esteem of childhood and the free play of children are preserved. The youngest children got a little lost in this message from the government. In Stortinget we raise this perspective. The youngest need small groups of children, they need to belong to a group of children, and they need a good time to get used to it. Not least, it is important for the youngest children that enough adults are present all day, and that there are enough kindergarten teachers in kindergarten. Then the Christian People's Party is pleased that all the parties in the committee are behind this proposal for bullying: «The government is asking the government to propose its own legislation on a safe environment for care and learning, cf. Section 9a of the Training Act.’ We agree that there is a need for further legal support for the relationship and anti- bullying work of the kindergarten. This is an important step in the further work on bullying even in kindergartens. Then to Prop. 103 L. Christian People's Party approves the Government's proposal for changes to Prop. 103 L, with only one exception. As regards the parental benefit exemption for children receiving special educational assistance, the Christian People's Party believes that it should continue. The Children’s Home Law Committee argued that reduced parental pay for this group of children could stimulate increased childcare participation. Childcare participation is likely to have a particularly positive effect on these children, and parental deductions may help to make the child’s needs for special educational assistance easier to see in relation to the child’s day-to-day childhood. For families with income above the income limit of the maternity schemes and with high expenditure, the removal of the reimbursement scheme would be of economic importance. We believe that this is unfortunate, and we are therefore promoting the following proposals: ‘The government asks the government to continue the scheme of deduction from the parental allowance for children receiving special educational assistance.’ Then in a later article I'll come back to other things in Prop. 103 L, e.g. what to do with the movement of the date limits and how to understand them. I also need to discuss in a later article how the Christian People's Party will vote in these matters.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e1417	Now almost all children in Norway go to kindergarten before they start school. SV is very proud to have contributed to the kindergarten reform that was carried out during our reign, which made it possible. It is perhaps the largest and most important promise for the Norwegian welfare state in many decades. It means a new everyday life for Norwegian children and for Norwegian families. Several children are given the opportunity to participate in the learning, the well-being, the development of curiosity, social competence and language, and the secure framework around the everyday life that kindergartens mean. It also means a simpler everyday life for families. This means that more people can participate in the world of work, and that means over time – I am convinced – social cohesion, because kindergarten is an arena to give equal opportunities to different children. It's an important arena for the fight against Difference Norway, to create a society that doesn't give up one child. It was a courageous political choice to implement the kindergarten reform. Like all major reforms, it was also demanding. Not least, the great lack of well-educated kindergarten teachers was a challenge. In spite of this, studies have shown that the quality of nursery services has kept up, despite the enormous expansion. It's thanks to the amazing efforts of employees, professionals and kindergarten owners laid down in this period. But although it is very positive to say whether what we have left behind, there is clearly a need for quality assurance and development of the quality of our kindergartens. That is why also the then state council Kristin Halvorsen promoted the future kindergarten in the spring of 2013. It is interesting to see that several of the proposals made in that communication are also followed up by the current government. In some areas, Torbjørn Røe Isaksen follows up good red-green kindergarten policy, and I am pleased about that. Already in the foreword to that announcement, Kristin Halvorsen affirmed the following: ‘The Norwegian nursery tradition is valuable. It assumes that childhood has a value in itself. It is important that children are fine here and now and have good experiences every day. In Norwegian kindergartens, play is the main starting point for learning.’ That view was a conscious choice of value. This is also the starting point for the concerns I and SV have related to the message we are now dealing with. That is why I asked the Minister of Knowledge earlier this spring to give an account of the department's lack of involvement of the profession even in the preparation of a new framework plan. So the Minister of Knowledge has repeatedly assured us that yes, he is also concerned about taking care of the Norwegian nursery model. But it's hard to believe it when several proposals go in the opposite direction to what we've built up our kindergartens to date. It concerns the proposal for the introduction of an indicative language standard. Language researchers say it's impossible to make a complete norm – that a state norm will be seen as an established measure of children's language in kindergarten, no matter what the government says. The proposal for a new framework plan to clarify the linguistic and social benefits of children after kindergarten is also a completely different way of thinking – a basic philosophy, a fundamental idea for the orientation of kindergarten. The exclusion of professional environments even in the work on a new framework plan is also an important breach of what has been one of the reasons why Norwegian kindergartens are as high in quality as they do. That is why I am pleased that today we can form a majority against the government on a number of these crucial points. This is also why SV is promoting its own proposal today, which makes even more concrete the order to the Council of State to include the profession in the work on a new framework plan. A good childhood lasts a lifetime. We have to spend the big money on the little people -- our children. This is the best investment in the future of Norway. But then we also have to let children be children, and don't go on the big misunderstanding that making kindergarten more like school is the right way to facilitate more learning. The right way to make sure that children succeed in school is, on the contrary, to let children be children and leave the game in the centre of kindergarten. With this, I'm going to take up the proposal that's been distributed in the hall, and just say that since the committee has given the other parties half of their speaking time, I can't even explain our vote, so I'm going to redraw it to do.

We don't plan to sit in a meeting room in the department and write a framework plan. We're going to engage and involve the profession. We will also take initiatives after this, including: together with the Education Association, to see if we can find some common arenas where we can get a discussion based on the majority in the Stortinge. Then there is much that gives a clear signal that we will continue to work with. And that's important things. It's about, for example, on a framework plan that has even clearer requirements and expectations for the work of kindergartens in language, so that we can capture those kindergartens that are falling out today. A framework plan that is clearer and a better tool – today it is also very extensive, perhaps something to do with it. A kindergarten framework that makes it more clear that there is a difference between the environment around a one-year-old and the environment around a five-year-old. A framework plan and a regulation that makes it clearer about the transition between kindergarten and school, which many municipalities get to very well, but where too many fail. And also legislation – to have a safety valve through independent supervision by the County, so that you can go in and check if parents, employees or the County itself believe that the municipality does not take its supervisory responsibility and does it well. The significance of a good start in life cannot be underestimated. I actually have two approaches to the kindergarten discussion. One is that today's kindergarten is viewed differently from before. Kinderhagen has been motivated by equality, with social economy because both parents can be out of work, and with the fight against Difference Norway. All that is important, and kindergarten contributes to all that, but above all, kindergarten is about the children who go there. For many years, the main priorities have been the number of places. Now the main priorities must be the content and quality of children. The second is that there are too many differences between kindergartens today. We can view it as variation in manning. We can look at the variation in the number of kindergarten teachers. It can be seen from the results of studies that show variations in the systematic, thorough and well-functioning educational work planned and carried out. The goal, of course, is not for those who work really well to work worse, but for us to get more kindergartens to work even better, and – I have to add – more kindergarten owners, both private and public, to work even better. That is why I believe that the framework plan is such an important tool. The framework plan needs to describe even more clearly than at present what kindergarten should be and provide a clearer framework for the important work being done. The framework plan should make it clear that there are differences between a one-year-old and a five-year-old — not through individual learning objectives, but with regard to the kind of environment the nursery should provide, for learning. So, of course, it's individual how children evolve, and it once again suggests that kindergarten should not become a school, and that we should not have individual learning goals. At the same time, we know that children need something to reach for, and the nursery will provide, but through play and learning hand in hand, as today. We know that many kindergarten children get help and support too late. The kindergarten gives us a unique opportunity to find the children who need help, and we have to do that through precision, not mass action. There is a reason why this government, unlike the previous government, has not proposed the monitoring of all four-year-olds, for example, who were in practice the consequence of previous government measures, precisely because it is the systematic observation, which the Workers' Party writes so well about in an article in the VG today, which is crucial, and then the measures are put in place for the children who need it, after a good professional assessment. Today's framework has many qualities, but it is unclear in several areas. We know that kindergartens interpret the regulation differently, and this in itself can contribute to the emergence of quality differences between kindergartens, for example. in the language work. And when we know how crucial language work is, not for the future's work or schooling or anything like that, but for play, for development, to be able to thrive and have fun and make friends in kindergarten, it's serious. Studies also show that there are too many differences between kindergartens, and this may affect the linguistic basis of the children at school. Therefore, more systematic and conscious language work is needed in kindergartens, and not least learning from the kindergartens that really do. Learning is not new in kindergarten, and I think one of the most important things that has happened in kindergarten is that the profession so clearly tells us that kindergarten is part of the education process, but a genuine educational arena other than school. Both in the current framework plan and in the 1996 framework plan, it is clear that there is to be learning in kindergarten. The question is, then, how. I would like to stress that the provision of more education in kindergarten does not mean that children should play less, and instead of having theoretical explanations about it, you can simply go around Barnehage-Norway and see how to stimulate the curiosity of children, how children play and learn at the same time, and how to find the good balance between the completely free game, where adults may have to intervene to make sure that everyone is involved, and the adult-driven, adult-nitted activities that are also play. We have to be careful to have a system that is looking for defects in children, but at the same time we have to have a system that ensures that children who need extra arrangements or other efforts get help as early as possible. In other words, good professional judgement in the profession must be combined with systematics and good practice. It's not an attack on the professional handling room of the profession. Nor is it, I believe, a time thief, a prerequisite for the pedagogical activities of kindergarten. Then it's up to the individual kindergarten to decide about, when and how to use educational tools. I have also noticed that the Storting wants a legal support for a safe care and learning environment, and will of course come back to Stortinget with it in an appropriate way. Then I have to admit that I am a little stunned when it comes to some of the rhetoric about recruitment standards. The reason for that is that there's one thing all in this room agree on, is that. What's in the government statement is exactly what it says, for example. Work party programme. If you're going to highlight someone here, it's the Christian People's Party, which has even invested money into the standard of manpower in its alternative state budget. Not all parties have. We agree that we should have a standard of personnel by 2020, and of course we should also follow up the Storting order to get a plan for that. But let me just stress that more important than a plan is following up the policy itself. When we took over the government offices, we lacked 4,400 kindergarten teachers. Now the number is halved. It's good for kindergarten, it's good for the kids, and it shows that evolution is going in the right direction. But of course we'll do more. I have said that we have to meet today's standard of education before we consider an enhanced one, but we have to follow the Storting order, of course. But also in this area, we need to be concerned not only with how many kindergarten teachers we are going to have, but also with what opportunities the kindergarten teachers have, for example. through a competence strategy further developed in this Communication. The further development of the strategy and its continuation are strongly supported, and I am pleased about that.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e1647	The experience of children in kindergarten today helps to lay the foundation for their later life opportunities. The content and quality of kindergarten are essential contributions to good school outcomes and a foundation for success in working and social life. The learning of children is a coherent process throughout the educational cycle. In kindergarten, the child’s learning ability and learning potential shall be met and maintained in a care and learning environment characterised by safety, inclusion and play. Knowledge and competence, skills and attitudes are developed in interaction with others in meaningful communities where children have the opportunity to express themselves. The play is the basic learning form of the child and must also have a prominent place in the kindergarten in the future. The kindergarten teacher shall facilitate good pedagogical processes that strengthen and support children’s learning and development through challenges and that are adapted to each child. The school shall be based on the foundation that children carry with them from kindergarten. In kindergarten and school, children and young people will meet a content that matters to their lives here and now, but at the same time lays a solid foundation for future social and work life. Children and young people shall be active participants in the learning process, they shall be challenged, and they shall experience the benefits and pleasure of solving increasingly advanced problems and finding satisfactory answers. From kindergarten, children shall have basic, good social and linguistic skills. At school, students will go deeper into professional content, concepts and contexts, and students will increasingly be able to use their skills to acquire more knowledge. Given how much a good kindergarten can mean for children, and how much resources in the form of public transfers and parental payments are used in the kindergarten sector in Norway, we should expect a good and high quality childcare provision. Low-quality childcare services affect the child’s safety, well-being, play, development and learning in everyday life and provide them with a poor starting point for further growth and schooling. High quality of the offer is important for children to have a good childhood, as well as for health promotion. A good Norwegian-speaking foundation is essential for children's further education, education and professional life. Minority-speaking children who have been in kindergarten for several years should be able to master Norwegian well enough at school to receive satisfactory benefits from school education. Contributing to the best possible linguistic basis for children at school is one of the most important tasks of kindergarten.

Then the way to change children's lives is also much shorter. It is anxiety, anxiety and anger that best describe the reactions that have occurred, after the government presented the issues where they put out a new course for kindergartens. That's a shame, because 97 pst. Among children from three to five years of age now in kindergarten, there is a formidable opportunity to balance differences. We know from research that early action and follow-up early in life are very crucial for how it goes later. We've had discussions in school policy about increasing efforts from first to fourth stages. We proposed a reading, writing and calculating guarantee for second-class ratings. We haven't had a majority for that. Nevertheless, the opposition parties the Christian People's Party and the Left have been permeated in the agreement on the budget for an effort that the government itself cannot afford: more teachers in the small school stage and more attention to the early efforts. We have long seen one in four students — for 20 years, coal after coal — end up in high school before they have completed and passed. We've been looking for reasons in the transition between high school and high school. We've been looking for new GIV and FYR projects – what it's all called – and transition projects, and it's got zero results. The reason is that this disqualification starts in children's learning very much earlier. It starts early in school, yes, for some people already in kindergarten that much of this foundation is laid. Therefore, there is full support in the Storting that kindergarten is a learning arena. Children have a great ability to learn when they play, and play when they learn, and of course this should be facilitated in kindergartens. The problem and where the Council of State goes wild is: what central management and what central direction do our good pedagogues in kindergarten need in order to actually do that job? It sometimes seems that you think about both school and kindergarten that if not the Education Directorate or the Department of Knowledge instruct people to do it one way or the other, there is no learning. But these are people who have years of education in teaching children, in taking care of children, in doing quality educational work, which are the ones who see the kids every day, who experience the social situations, who know home and parents, and who have to use their experience, their knowledge and their education to create good opportunities for all children. Had the Council of State listened to the sector, to those who know the most about the development of children and what works in kindergartens, he would have realized that those in the sector do not believe that some of the new initiatives of the government will lead to more quality. On the contrary, there is a warning against several of them. The government's answer is that no, it wasn't really meant to be that way, it's really the things they're already doing, the screening tests, the tools that already exist, that's what's to be used. It must strike the council that it is very strange that there will be so much conflict if he really just suggests that things should continue as before. Somewhere this conflict must come from. It may be the training association's fault that there will be a conflict about this, that there will be a conflict about the absence limit in the student organisation, or that there will be a conflict of competence between teachers' organisations. However, when there is conflict in areas by area, with the sector, it may also have something to do with the work of the Ministry of Knowledge, not working closely enough on them, not getting an understanding of reforms or perhaps not listening and finding these more agreed solutions that are thus also being implemented. The sector itself says that they essentially don't need so many more tools. They need, first of all, time and resources to use the tools that exist. It doesn't help much, says the sector, that we need to have even more tools to monitor that students or kindergarten kids are struggling with reading or language, if we don't have time to take the consequences of students struggling with reading or language. A good parallel is one thing we have in school, where the minister of knowledge a month and a half ago was on the TV 2 news, deeply shocked that half of the kids in school go out without being able to swim, and where we are here in the hall, for example. have proposed the introduction of an obligation for all schools and municipalities to teach young people how to swim. It was voted down by the Right. What is Right's answer instead? Well, putting in a screening test on whether the students can swim. But the mapping is there. The background for the whole news item was that half could not swim, and it takes the teachers approx. Five seconds to figure out if the kids can swim or not, it's just to bring them in the water, you see it right away. What's missing is time to teach them to swim, time to teach. It's this thing that's the whole difference between the left and right school and kindergarten policies, that you think you can only measure to better quality. One is extremely concerned about measuring the results in school, but one has to start to focus on creating results in school, and in the same way we have to think about kindergarten policy. You don't just have to measure the results. You have to create results. You have to make sure that the pedagogical staff, all the amazing professionals and assistants who work with the kids every day, actually get the job they're very aware of. The children's gardening effort of the Stoltenberg government helped to give us a unique opportunity. Here we can discover the children who have come out wrong for some reason. Here we can discover the children who, if we don't help them, may be striving for the rest of their lives. We can find them early. We can assist them early. We can give them a completely different start. But how are we going to find these kids if we don't have enough adults to look for? How are we going to find these challenges and help them get started better if we don't have time to help? The working party has good solutions for how this is to be done. Point one: get enough competent adults in place, so that every child is seen, and so that adults in a professional environment can assess who needs extra help and provide the assistance needed. Point two: ensure that kindergartens have good plans for the systematic follow-up of those who need it. This can be done by spending a little less time on controversial mass-monitoring tools and, instead, requiring all kindergartens to develop a plan that ensures systematic observation, assessment and follow-up of children’s development, such as: the new nursery agency from the Workers' Party is doing in Oslo. When a child is discovered hanging out, the nursery chooses the best screening tool they think is. They identify the children the pedagogical leader thinks they need it, and put in place the follow-up they think the child needs, and parents and supervisors should be involved all the way – unbureaucratic, the resources put in where they are needed, they get to do the job they are assigned to, trust and flexibility that helps young people. It also means that we do not use resources on unnecessary forms, mapping and bureaucracy. We know that this government seems to have its own goal of increasing the number of bureaucrats as much as possible. We don't share that goal, but we think that resources need to be spent on learning, on quality and on development. The invitation from the Workers' Party to the Minister of Knowledge is: Use the knowledge that lies there, all the amazing employees, the recordings that come from there. Then we create a kindergarten that can work well for everyone.
