ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e1816	For Prop. 103 L for 2015–2016: It is up to the municipality to decide how to organise the admission of children born in September or October. The municipality's obligation is to provide space from the month when the child reaches the age of one, and the child's right is to have a place in the nursery in the municipality in which it is resident, from the month when the child reaches the age of one year. The municipality may choose to provide these children with space during August, but it is important to stress that the municipality does not have the possibility to require start-up and to require parental payment from August onwards by parents with children born in September or October. It is up to the kindergarten or municipality to determine which day during the month – i.e. the month when the child reaches the age of one – the child can start, and the parents will be obliged to pay the parental payment from the starting date set by the municipality or kindergarten, provided that it is within the month when the child reaches the age of one year. It is also important to stress that the purpose of the extension of the right to kindergarten is to ensure that several one-year-olds have children's playgrounds, not to facilitate or provide incentives for former kindergartens for children under one year of age. It is stated in the government's consultation note that kindergartens must: «have a higher density of kindergarten teachers during the period August to October than the minimum requirement laid down in the pedagogical norm in order to receive children during September/October». Furthermore, it is written: ‘However, a higher density of employees in August/September will be an advantage in view of the quality of kindergartens. In August there will be many new children in most kindergartens. Several kindergarten teachers and employees per child can contribute to better follow-up opportunities for all children in a familiarisation phase.» This legislative decision shall facilitate the possibility for several one-year-olds to start in kindergarten when they need space, and the financing of this is a state responsibility. Additional expenditure on kindergartens and municipalities is covered by the state budget. I just want to clarify it, because there have been some signs that you have not fully understood what this movement of the date limit has meant, and that this will result in greater costs for the municipalities. It is important to clarify that this is not to be burdened on either kindergarten or municipality; this is to be granted under the State budget. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e1913	The case rapporteur will probably present the setting. I will present the matter from the centre party's point of view. Firstly, this matter is extremely important, because clean, safe food and safe drinks are incredibly important for public health. There is a happy development in Norwegian opinion, which is that more and more people are becoming aware of the need to make greater demands on food. Many of the lifestyle diseases and public health problems we have are based on the fact that food and drink contain substances that over time cause serious complications. Point two: The matter is serious because what has been the Big City's long-term objectives has not been taken seriously, and is not taken seriously, by the Ministry of Agriculture and Food. I am very pleased with the report from the Riksrevision that goes into a topic and assesses whether what has been said in this room over 16 years, is well, has been followed up or not. It's commendable, and it's the Riks revision in honor. The size has been a target for organic farming since 1999. Then it was said that the goal was that 10 pst. of the total agricultural area should have been converted into organic farming by 2010. In 2005, the target was changed to 15 pst. production and consumption should be organic by 2015. So one saw that it was hard to reach, so in 2010 the time period was extended to 2020. We have precise objectives. The objectives are so precise that organic food production is to be carried out at 15 pst. of the total agricultural area, and it is clearly defined what agricultural area is, it is perfectly clear to the Ministry of Agriculture and Food in its statistics. It says 15 pst. the organic nature of the livestock. Pets also know the Ministry of Agriculture and Food what is in relation to wild animals. So we're talking about domestics. It's ambitious, it all agrees, but the Storting was aware that it was ambitious goals when the goals were set. What I am now saying is that there is a unanimous committee behind it: the precision of the objectives, that it is ambitious, and that the Storting was aware that it was ambitious, when the objectives were set. Organic farming is defined in the organic production legislation of the organic production and labelling regulation of organic agricultural products and foodstuffs of 4 October 2005, the so-called organic regulation. All producers of organic agricultural products and foodstuffs must comply with public legislation in this area. So it's exactly regulated. This ecological mode of operation is based on a number of assumptions. Natural resources shall be managed in such a way as to avoid harmful effects on the environment, thereby ensuring the long-term fertility of the soil. Genetic diversity and species diversity must be ensured, and organic farming must create an environment that meets the natural behaviour and needs of livestock. Then comes what is the most difficult and most demanding, namely that there should be a shift of growth that should be as varied as possible, and plant cultivation should be set up to prevent weeds, diseases and pests, and to minimise the recycling of organic material. It is very demanding to farm organically with the least possible weeds. Anyone who's tried to practice it knows that. Needles are easier to use. Organic farming provides for a balance between land and livestock farming with regard to the production of feed and the use of manure – glittering. It is old knowledge that there must be consistency between the livestock that you have, that lives on plants, and the area on which you have to grow plants – good, old, value-conservative knowledge that organic farming has now taken on. It is to their credit. Organic farming aims to ensure that products are based on the overall starting point even after processing. Processing is therefore also essential. What you put into the raw materials, how you process them, is also essential, for anyone who reads a product information, sees all the substances that are put into it one should eat, after it has left the primary agriculture. Furthermore, in organic farming there is a requirement that genetically modified organisms and products derived therefrom should not be used. Genetically modified organisms, if we take plants for example, are plants that tolerate sprays that are of such a nature that common plants are killed for feet if this spray is used. In the case of genetically modified plants, the plant tolerates a spray from which all other green plants die. It shows what that plant contains, and that is the professional, factual reason why one should stay away from genetically modified organisms and genetically modified plants. It is the Ministry of Agriculture and Food that has overall responsibility for agricultural management and shall facilitate the achievement of the objectives of organic farming. There has never been any doubt as to who is responsible for this matter. It is the Ministry of Agriculture and Food at the Council of State. The latest public, documented figures are found in the 2016 Agriculture Directorate's report, and it shows that there is still a decline in the ecological area in Norway. That happens in 2015, and there's now a situation where only 4.8 pst. of the total Norwegian agricultural area is ecological area. Overall, it is at the lower edge of 500000 dekars. So it's going down. The share of organic cereal production is about 1 pst. The proportion of organically sold power feed was 1,8 pst. in 2015, and organic livestock production in 2015 was the following in volume: milk 3,4 pst., meat 1 pst., poultry 0,2 pst., eggs 5,2 pst. In the case of fruit, berries and vegetables, which are occupied by a lot of people, it turns out that there is also the area in decline, but that it is mainly due to the decline in the potato area. The total ecological fruit, berries and vegetable area is only 2,3 pst. of total area. What about the consumption? There's a radical change in people's consumption habits, which I started with. Suffering from the fact that in the grocery trade the organic food was only 1.45 p.m. in 2014, there has been an increase of almost 30 p.m. from 2013 to 2014. - It's huge. According to the Directorate of Agriculture's Report No. 12 from 2016 increased the turnover of organic food and drink by nearly 15 pst. in 2015, to nearly 2 billion. kr, and it is a doubling since 2010. In channels other than the retail trade, it also increased significantly. So what happens is a positive attitude change in Norwegian consumers to organic food and drink, and that's good. That is very good, and here I would like to address a call to the food chain. The daily food chains with the monopolist market that the three have now have a great responsibility to do a job to obtain more organic products for a decent price to consumers, so that one can get a development that is consistent with the Storting's will in that area. There has been a problem so far that there has been more production than what has been traded on the market. This has led to a large proportion of organic production being sold as ordinary products in the market. From 2010, the share of organic products resold from reception and processing plants as organically labelled products has increased for all livestock products. But still it's like that in 2015 that only 54 pst. organically produced milk is sold as organic. For meat the equivalent is 39 pst. and meat means bovine, ovine, lamb and porcine. For poultry, it's 100 pst. – happy – for eggs 81 pst. Now, what's noticing here is that evolution is going in the right direction, but still there's too much of what's produced organically, that's being sold as ordinary goods. The National Audit Office has pointed out five main findings, and the most important one is given in point 1: ‘The development of organic production and consumption is not in accordance with the size of the site.’; It also states that the legislation is too complex and unwieldy, incentives are not enough to increase organic production, and governance has not provided sufficient facilities to increase public consumption of organic food. This applies to the marketing, where the generic marketing, which is in good Norwegian terms the marketing of a product category instead of a particular brand, is not extensive enough to reach the consumer, distributor, wholesale and retail trade. Why are we doing organic farming? There are two main reasons for that. The first is that consumers who want to eat and drink organic agricultural products should have their needs met through national production for the products naturally produced in Norway. The second is that organic farming should be a learning area for Norwegian agriculture as a whole in order to make farming methods more forward-looking by promoting soil fertility and improving animal welfare. Thus, meeting consumer needs and improving the operation of Norwegian agriculture as a whole are very good purposes. Norwegian agriculture is passed through. Norwegian agriculture is a private industry. It is a business, but it has been passed. I do not know any private industries that are as pervasive as Norwegian agriculture. It is governed by laws, regulations and not least by economic instruments. More than the entire net income in Norwegian agriculture comes from the state budget, and I can assure the President that it will not be distributed a krone by the state without any intention behind it. It's passed. Then why are we in that situation, when you have so many tools, that you're so far away from the goal? This fundamental imbalance between the objective and that set out in this national audit report could not be a coincidence. The size has been target for organic farming since 1999, when it was set to target 10 pst. of the total agricultural area should have been converted into organic farming by 2010. In 2005, the target was changed to 15 pst. production and consumption should be organic by 2015. In 2010, the period was extended to 2020. The majority of the Committee has noted that organic legislation is a matter that the Riksrevision points out as a possible reason why organic food production does not have a greater market share. The national audit highlights the complexity and intransigence of the legislation. The organic production, further processing and labelling legislation is part of the EEA Agreement. The current regulation on organic production and labelling of organic agricultural products and foodstuffs of 2005 — the Ecology Regulation — is based on an EU regulation of 1991 and a number of amending and implementing regulations. The majority of the Committee assumes that the main reason why the organic food production target is not met is that the demand for organic food is too low in Norway. Today, parts of organic production must be sold as ordinary products. The majority point to Norwegian-produced food as a whole having a reputation for being safe to eat, probably much because Norwegian agriculture is at a world level in terms of purity in the production process. Among other things, low antibiotics are used in Norwegian livestock production, which helps Norwegian agriculture to be lowly exposed to antibiotic resistance. It can also help explain why conventionally grown food is so good in relation to organic food. In this context, price also plays a crucial role. Organic food is generally more expensive than conventional food. An overall committee points out that the Riksrevision recommends that the Ministry of Agriculture and Food developing an up-to-date, comprehensive strategy to achieve the objectives of organic production and consumption that the party to the agricultural settlement provides incentives that contribute more to increasing organic production takes the initiative to increase the purchase of organic food in the public use generic marketing and knowledge-based information on organic farming in order to increase the consumption of organic food; The majority of the Committee points out that the Council of State agrees with the Riksrevision that a new and comprehensive strategy is needed, and that one will then consider how and what initiatives are to be taken towards other departments and activities to promote organic food in private and organic mass households. The majority have noted that the Council of State believes it is resource-intensive to influence consumers to choose organic food through generic marketing campaigns, and that instead, one should focus on information for consumers in the further work on the subject. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e2395	The Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs is again presenting a position on the development and use of digital services in public life and in public services. So this time it's about the development and use in our municipalities. The setting is unanimous. The Committee's view on digitisation, position and use in the public sector has been unanimous in all the positions we have delivered by the Control and Constitutional Committee. The Committee has addressed several serious issues relating to key areas of Norwegian society. The last time we were treated was the digitization in Nav, and then the digitization within our health companies. The latter led to a unanimous decision by the Stortinget that the Council of State should return to Stortinget in an appropriate manner on the further development of the health sector. The government has responded to this decision by submitting the notification, which is called the Digital Agenda for Norway, and I think that is good. However, there has been no lack of communications and proper objectives, but in all these cases dealt with by the Control and Constitutional Committee, there is one main issue that remains, and there is a lack of coordination and overall governance. I say this again: there is a lack of coordination and overall governance that is what is left in the attitude of the Control and Constitutional Committee in this important area of society. It is also the main theme of the issue that Stortinget is dealing with today, i.e. the digitisation in the municipalities. Here too, like the matter we have just dealt with, the goal and decision of the Storting is quite clear. That's what it says. «total and complete public digital services for residents and businesses». That's the goal that the Storting has set, also for today's discussion. In this area, the municipalities have not fulfilled this obligation either. The size has joined several times and pointed out that the local government sector has a separate responsibility towards its citizens in order to solve basic tasks, provide services, drive social development, exercise powers and be a local political institution. The fact that citizens and businesses have to be able to carry out their duties in the public sector in an easy way, regardless of whether it is the State, the county or the municipality responsible for the service, is part of this work. Digitization is a prerequisite for this in our time. I think everyone here agrees with what I have just said. It is, of course, discouraging that the work on digitisation in the municipalities has not come any further than we can read in the Riksrevision reports. The national audit studies show that municipalities have been short-lived, that there are considerable differences between municipalities and that the differences between large and small municipalities are striking. The Municipal People’s Council implies that each municipality has a separate responsibility for the renewal, conversion and digitisation of its own municipality, but national targets for equivalent services and the efficient and coordinated use of public resources are equally important. The state is also entrusted with a significant responsibility for giving municipalities the opportunity to digitise. The National Audit Office considers that the measures undertaken by the State are not sufficient to achieve the objective of comprehensive digital public services for citizens and businesses, and that there is a need for stronger nationally coordinated efforts to improve the progress of municipal digitisation. The National Audit Office recommends that the Ministry of Local Government and Modernisation assume a strong national responsibility for coordinating ICT policies in the public sector, so that public digital services can be seen as a whole for citizens and businesses. They propose more use of financial incentives, motivating the coordination of departments and directorates, facilitating coordination between the State and local authorities, and increasing the use of national common components and ICT standards by the municipality in order to streamline the digitisation of municipal services. The Committee agrees. I am pleased that the Committee is united in this matter, and that the Committee stresses the importance of taking seriously the findings of the National Audit Office. We believe it is serious that the Storting's objective of comprehensive and complete public digital services to the population and the business community is not met. It creates inequality and increased differences for both the population and the business community when online services are not a common good in all the municipalities of the country. Better interaction between the state and the municipality is absolutely necessary, and we stress the responsibility of the Ministry of Municipal Affairs and the Ministry of Modernisation. It goes without saying that good online solutions between the municipality and the population will contribute both to shorter processing times and to better resource use. I think most people appreciate being able to process a construction case online rather than visiting public offices. Construction work is also a good example in another respect. The Building Quality Directorate has developed a solution for building applications that have been used by many municipalities, and in this way has actually done what many municipalities demand, got to a greater degree of state involvement and responsibility, because it goes without saying that the challenges ahead, and already today, are too great for the municipalities to be able to solve them alone. It points to one side that I, for my part, think is striking in many things we are dealing with in Stortinget – the inability to learn from others. This applies to both the State and the municipality. It's in many ways a mystery that you don't go out and see what others have done when new solutions are to be introduced. When digital solutions are missing, there are several very unfortunate consequences. Data across subject areas and subject systems are not working. Inhabitants and businesses need to re-enter the same information for each of the municipal services. This leads to an improper offer, and it is not least to waste time and resources on people, businesses and municipalities. It is worrying when many municipalities say that they do not have the skills to digitise their services. Not least, it is worrying that municipalities do not have sufficient ordering skills. The municipalities can handle, inter alia: not to acquire ICT systems or data solutions in line with the local authority’s own needs. As I just said, there is little coordination and standardisation of the solutions that need to be addressed. It is essential to ensure that systems communicate with existing solutions and that data flows and interactions between those systems are ensured. Many municipalities say that the cost of digitising municipal services is too high for the municipality to prioritise it, and that the work is also lacking in the management of the municipality. It may not be so strange all the time that costs are on the municipalities, while the profits are on the community level. A Joint Committee will therefore stress that the findings need to be taken seriously and corrected, and that municipalities need to cooperate more closely and share experience and technological solutions. We also show that the Ministry of Local Government and Modernisation has the overall responsibility for coordinating ICT policy, and we therefore agree that there is a need for stronger nationally coordinated efforts to improve the progress of local authorities in digitising municipal services.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e2435	Public sector digitisation shall enhance the quality and efficiency of public service activities. Municipalities have little digitised their services. Work on digitisation of municipal services has significant obstacles. However, citizens and businesses are offered a digital first choice for only 27 pst. of the municipal services in three key service areas. According to the Riksrevision, many municipalities stress that the costs of digitising municipal services are too high for the municipalities to prioritize it, and if this work is not sufficiently anchored in management, it tends to stop, with a sluggish system as a result. When municipalities are not in direct contact with national registers, but instead make local copies such as shadow registers, the quality of the work will not be good enough, as shadow registers are not updated often enough. Unfortunately, this has been a well-known issue for many years. The Directorate for Administration and ICT, Diffi, has contributed to the digitisation of the public sector, but it is the central government that is the main target group of diffi. Divi is therefore limited in its ability to address cross-governmental and municipal issues. Many municipalities lack knowledge of the importance of ICT standards and architecture principles for the effective digitisation of municipal services. Since the national architecture principles are recommended only for the municipal sector, while they are mandatory for the State, the challenge is reinforced in order to achieve a good interaction between the municipal level and the state level. When a large majority of municipalities, according to the report of the Riksrevision, believe that national authorities should commit municipalities more in digitisation work, it is also close to seeing that common architecture principles for the state and municipal sectors are necessary. A stronger initiative must be taken for the development of solutions that can be used across municipal borders and between municipalities and county and state agencies. Finally, I would like to express a great understanding of the situation of the municipalities. Both technically and economically, the digitisation of municipal services is difficult and complicated. The State must provide both financial resources and competence to enable municipalities to better meet the findings of the National Audit Office’s report. In addition, it should be necessary that the principles of architecture, which are mandatory for State activities, are also made compulsory and applicable to municipalities. Otherwise, I am afraid that we will continue to experience so great technical challenges that the goal of digitisation of municipal services cannot be achieved in a long time.

Over the years, there has been a lot of discussion about this particular issue — about one-day choices or two-day choices. What I'm a little curious about is that you're so eager to remove this minority right. I think the argument used is in principle, and you also use economic arguments. About the last thing I want to say is that I think of it as purely constructed reasoning. It is not a question of getting cheaper or more expensive when it comes to implementing elections in Norway every two years. It is a reconstructed argument to reinforce the argument that one should not have the same right that one has had in setting up two-day elections. I really don't think the majority of the Storting should have done this. A field that has been established for a long time, where minority rights in this important area have been taken into account. Sometimes the Workers' Party has been part of the minority rights of local government, and sometimes there have been others who have been in it. The minority right has helped to ensure that the local assessment of a minority in the local council has given the possibility to set up a two-day election. There has been no problem, so I really don't understand why you're doing this. You can add the basic arguments if you want to, but you don't want to do it here. This, I think, is – it may not be such a word to be used from the Parliament's pulpit, President, so if there's any knocking, I'll take it easy – uglyly done. That is simply the word bad – I think it is bad, because it is not necessary to do this. There can be no other purpose to this than to make it more difficult to get two-day choices. It has to be when a minority right disappears — that makes it harder to get two-day choices. Then the intention cannot be any other. For the principles attached to this, it is not necessary to indicate in the contexts established here. I record that, for our part – the Workers' Party, which was originally fighting through this scheme – we are now being downgraded, and that we must, of course, set ourselves up after the Storting has said this. But as I said, we should not have done this. It is unnecessary to do so, and the arguments put forward in the mark are formal, and this formality should not have been used to use the possibility of obtaining good two-day elections in the municipalities. That's what I'm talking about.

In all other cases, it is true that those who are going to charge the municipal fund expenditure – it must be a majority – are also those who are going to balance the budget and make room for these expenditures according to a local priority order. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e2783	The changes we are considering today in the law of division and the municipal law have been justified by the ministry's desire to make the merger processes resulting from the municipal reform more flexible. The Committee has noted that in the opinions received by the Ministry for its consultation note, there are different assessments of the proposed legislative changes. The Committee has therefore provided a review of each proposal in order to assess the principal aspects of the proposed legislative changes. Some of the proposals have been considered by an overall committee to be appropriate and unproblematic. Other of the proposals are the different evaluations of the Committee and the majority and minority voting. The Committee agrees with the Ministry's proposal on how to treat merged municipalities in ROBEK if one or more of the municipalities is in the register because they are in financial imbalances. We agree that a new and overall assessment should be carried out after aggregation. The Committee also agrees with the Ministry's proposal that the provisions of the Shareholders' Act on the right of shareholders to take over a share that changes ownership shall not apply when shares are transferred from a municipality to a new municipality. The Ministry's proposal for the division and alignment of borders in the division law is more problematic and has shared the Committee. The proposal requires the application of border adjustment legislation in areas with less than 10 pst. by the inhabitants and that it can be used for areas up to 25 pst. by the inhabitants. By treating border change cases as border adjustment and not sharing, the threshold of involvement and community involvement is lowered. I, together with the majority of the Committee, believe that issues related to classification and border adjustments are of great importance to the community and the people affected. It will also be the case that cross-border communities covering large parts of the municipality will be included in the proposed provisions on what is a smaller part of the municipality. We therefore do not support the Ministry's proposal for amendments to Section 3 of the Division Act. The Committee supports the Ministry's proposal to allow derogations from the requirement for the county council to be called the joint municipal board meeting after a decision on municipal merger has been taken. Where voluntary merger decisions have been taken and the municipalities have already agreed on all practical aspects of the merger, there is no need for a joint joint municipal board meeting. Where aggregation has been decided by the Storing without local consent, the requirement for the joint meeting will continue to apply. The Committee has shared in the evaluation of the Ministry's proposal to formalise the use of Section 17 of the Division Act, which provides for the new Municipal Council to be elected by and among the members of the Municipal Councils of the merged municipalities. The minority, the Central Party, the Workers' Party and the SV share the opposition of the KS to the proposal on the grounds that indirect elections are less democratic than the new election of local government. I also believe that there is every reason to stop the opinions of Professors Bernt and Smith who are both negative to the proposal to formalise the use of indirectly elected local government after a municipal merger. Professor Smith refers to the recently adopted change in the Constitution, where it is now the Constitution that citizens have the right to direct local affairs through local elected bodies. He believes that any statutory requirement to derogate from the ordinary rules on election to local government must be examined in more detail before any decision can be taken by the Stortinget. The Committee also shares the principles of governance of municipal enterprises in parliamentaryly governed municipalities. The Ministry wants to amend the Local Government Act in such a way that the local government/ county council is given the power to delegate greater powers to the local/local council. Also with regard to this proposal, I and the minority agree with the KS, which says this proposed amendment of the law could weaken the political and democratic governance of municipal enterprises. A unanimous committee agrees with the Ministry's proposal on the obligation to publish meetings. We agree that meetings that can also be held for closed doors and that relate to municipal enterprises are to be announced. Finally, the proposal on who can be elected as a member of the common denominator for the implementation of the agreed local or county merger. An overall committee agrees with the amendment of the law, which also allows members of local or county councils to be elected. The composition of the Municipal and Administrative Committee is such that the majority of the Committee does not necessarily give a majority in the Chamber. In this case, the changes concerning sharing and border adjustment in Section 3 of the Division Act, where a minority proposal has been made, which I expect they themselves to explain. Finally, according to the suspension, the Centerpartiet will not support changes to Section 17 of the Splitting Act and the Municipal Act §§ 62 and 70. Finally, I would like to thank the Committee on Good Cooperation.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e2815	Thanks to the rapporteur for his presentation on a case which has, on several points, brought together a unanimous committee, but which in some areas also makes some surprising minorities visible. When municipalities or county municipalities want to merge, it is important that we have sensible, predictable and flexible rules. The same applies in the case of the division of municipalities, or in the case of the adjustment of the borders, e.g.: where a branch of a municipality wishes to notify transfer to the neighbouring municipality. In the course of the municipal reform, the Ministry has also made many clarifications about issues that arise in the course of local negotiations — from what will happen with a differentiated employer’s fee to local hunting and fishing rights. The present case also clarifies what is happening to a ROBEK municipality, as the case-rapporteur explained, which goes with another municipality. There, the County now has the authority to assess whether ROBEK registration is still available – a practical solution, which was not directly clarified in the law before, but which is now. In other areas, the consensus of the Committee, as the rapporteur also pointed out, is not the same. Today, for example. border adjustments cannot be made at the same time as local government mergers. The question is not whether the ministry should be able to decide on border adjustments, because the ministry can today, the question is whether it should also be possible to decide on such changes in the context of a merger, as a majority of the committee – albeit not in the Chamber – disagrees for slightly unclear reasons. The proposal implies that some limit changes may be treated as limit adjustments, which can then be determined by the King, and may not be dealt with in the Storting as long as there is otherwise agreement on aggregation and division. Since the majority of the Committee is as the rapporteur put it, I would like to refer to proposal No. 1 in the option, on behalf of Right, Party of Progress and Left. Furthermore, the Ministry proposes to enter into the law the practice that has been followed for many years, that in a merged municipality, or in a merged state, there should be no exceptional choice if the aggregation is effective from a turn of the year as there is no prior choice in the harvest. The whole or part of the existing municipal or county councils then constitute the new elected body until elections are held. This is, therefore, a voluntary temporary arrangement with elected representatives who form a new elected body, and, contrary to what the rapporteur suggested, is well in line with the constitutional changes adopted by this House. This is not, as the setting says, an extended right to indirect selection, as the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and SV claim. On the other hand, it is a practice that has been followed for years, even under the same government as the three mentioned batches. Now it is explicitly suggested that this possibility be included in the law, and there are issues where the municipalities themselves, or the counties locally, agree not to hold extraordinary elections. Does the Workers' Party believe that the merger of Harstad and Bjarkøy in 2013 should not happen without extraordinary choice first? Their own government chose to do so in the way that they today oppose inclusion in the law. Do they disagree in their own procedure from then on, as seen afterwards? Does the Workers' Party believe that the merger of Sandefjord, Stokke and Andebu will not be carried out from the coming turn of the year without prior elections, when the municipalities agree? Does the Workers' Party believe that Rissa and Leksvik or Holmestrand and Hof or Lardal and Larvik will not be able to join forces from 1 January 2018 without having to hold extraordinary elections first? All of these municipalities have applied for aggregation and agree to set up new local authorities until the next ordinary elections. Why is the Labour Party voting today against the practice they themselves have followed being clarified in the form of an explicit law reference? Already in the morning, this room will deal with a matter of merging the Trondelags. The completions have decided to put together a new common completion from the merger date to the next choice. But in the present case, it is difficult to interpret the vote of the Labour Party, and insofar as the Senterpartiet and SVs, against the proposed confirmation of a long-term practice in a way other than that they believe that the wishes of the Turkish states should not be fulfilled, but that they should be extraordinarily elected. It would have been useful if the Labour Party in particular could clarify its position on this matter from this pulpit, and if they have not prepared a reply today, I can gladly inform you that the question will be repeated in tomorrow's debate.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e3010	It is a unanimous committee that subscribes to the legislative proposal under consideration. The proposal will allow the establishment of private minority ownership in jointly liable public water power companies or jointly liable companies, as an alternative to public limited liability companies. These changes allow private joint owners to take effect corresponding to their own holding as dividends. The Committee considers it important that this legislative amendment can contribute to a stable access to power for power-generating industries under commercial conditions. The Committee considers that the legislative changes do not prejudge the requirement of effective public ownership of the hydropower resources and therefore assume that the concessional treatment will carefully assess whether the company agreement allows public majority owners to exercise ownership in order to comply with the provisions of the law. Industrial ownership in the form currently open ensures self-sufficiency in industrial production and reduces the need for external power contracts. This will make industry less vulnerable to fluctuations on the power exchange. The Committee has not addressed the issues raised by the fact that proposals for a methodology for assessing the self-sufficiency of industry for the purposes of land-interest tax have now been put to the consultation. For my part, I would like to point out that the purpose of the amendment of the law that we are now doing is to ensure that industrial owners can meet their needs for foreseeable power availability. Predictability and long-termity are important for power-processing industries. Today's legislative change will contribute to this, and I expect that it will not be affected by other changes in the industry's framework conditions in this area.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-07.ana.ud158e3023	When I choose to take the floor even though it's a unanimous committee, and although it's kind of piggy in the press department, it's because I think this is in many ways a historic day. The Industrial Concession Act is a law that has been with us since we began to harvest industrially from our hydropower resources and has laid the foundation for good and efficient management and industrialisation based on our fantastic renewable hydropower resources. I am pleased that we have full political agreement on this matter, and that it has been clear from the time this matter was presented. This is an important issue for industry. Facilitating the continued development of the power-generating industry in Norway is important in order to continue to create large – and maximum – values based on our fantastic renewable hydropower resources, it is important to create profitable jobs, it is important in a climate perspective, and it is important to meet the not insignificant surplus of power we see. With this bill, the government follows up the Sundvolden Declaration. We are doing this to facilitate precisely what we have promised there, that industrial owners can own hydropower in a way that ensures the need for long-term, stable and predictable power availability from a generation perspective, while at the same time taking place within the consolidation model. I am pleased that the case-rapporteur presented the matter in a large way and also pointed out that with this bill we take care that those in power generation can take it out in force, instead of – as today – just as financial dividends, while at the same time ensuring that the public majority shareholder still has full control and ownership of the falls, which is the element that guarantees that we are good in European competition law with these changes. I am also pleased that, in the context of the public change of words about this matter, the group leader of Hydro Aluminium, Svein Richard Brandtzæg, said that due to this amendment that is being adopted today, after the government made the proposal earlier this year, it has been decided to continue the development of Hydro Karmøy and a new melter. It's one of the biggest investments in the mainland industry in a very, very long time. It is an investment that already creates hundreds of jobs, based on clean renewable energy, in a region that is now really in a situation where new activity and new profitable jobs are needed. So I just want to thank the case rapporteur and the rest of the Stortinget for a very good and very effective, you could say, cooperation on this matter.

The basis for a regulation is the expected wage growth in the regulatory year, adjusted for any deviation between expected and actual wage growth in recent years. The rule implies that the basic amount and the retirement pension are regulated in accordance with salary growth. Thus disability benefits and other benefits that are regulated in line with changes in the basic amount are regulated in accordance with the wage growth. It is similar for other pension schemes. For an old-age pension during payment, other rules apply. That is often what the discussion in this case has been about. This was determined by The Storting in 2007 and in practice implies that the old-age pension during payment is to be regulated by an average wage and price increase, less a fixed factor of 0,75 pst. The government supports this model. This is a model that is set out in the Sundvolden Declaration and is the basis of a large majority in the Storting. For 2016, the result of the negotiations is that the basic amount increases from 90 068 kroner to 92 576 kroner from 1 May 2016. This is an increase of $2,508, which is 2.78 pst.
